> @PredictiveHistory I think you're wrong in one part...  
> 18:50 "... the rats were not competing for food, they were really competing for status ..."   
>   
> This theory is contradicted to what I found  many years ago in Edward T. Hall's book "The Hidden Dimension" from 1966.  
>   
> One of the experiments described by Edward T. Hall in his book "The Hidden Dimension" references the work of John B. Calhoun, who conducted studies on rat populations in controlled environments he called "rodent universes." In those experiments, rats were provided with unlimited food, water, bedding, and shelter, essentially creating a "rat utopia" with no predators or scarcity of resources. However, the only limiting factor was space. As the rat population grew within this confined space, profound behavioral changes and social breakdown occurred, leading to a phenomenon Calhoun called the "behavioral sink." This sink was characterized by extreme disruptions in normal rat behaviors such as mating, nesting, and rearing of young. The rats exhibited abnormal and pathological behaviors, including hyper-aggression, neglect of offspring, increased infant mortality (up to 96% in some areas), cannibalism, and abandonment of social norms. Dominant males became overly aggressive, attacking females and young, while subordinate males sometimes became passive or exhibited abnormal sexual behaviors. The physiological consequences of the crowding stress were visible in the enlarged adrenal glands, kidneys, livers, and tumors found in the rats. Eventually, the population growth stopped, and population numbers plummeted due to the collapse of social organization and reproductive failure. This collapse happened even though food and water were unlimited, indicating that the limitation of space and the resulting psychological stress were critical factors in population demise. Hall notes that the rats seemed to maintain much lower population densities naturally by forming small groups with spacing between them. But when confined and crowded artificially, these social mechanisms collapsed, leading to the catastrophic outcomes observed. In summary, the experiment demonstrates that in animal populations (and by extension possibly humans), unlimited food and water are insufficient for healthy social and reproductive behavior if the spatial environment becomes overly crowded. Overcrowding caused behavioral disintegration and a population crash due to social and psychological stress rather than resource scarcity.This aligns with Hall's exploration of personal and social space in "The Hidden Dimension," emphasizing the critical role of spatial relationships in behavior and societal health.

>
> The Lifecycle of Civilizations (Stages of the State) according to Ibn Khaldun:  
>   
> In his magnum opus, the Muqaddimah, Ibn Khaldun likened the lifespan of a civilization (or dynasty) to that of a living organism. It is born, it grows to maturity, it ages, and it eventually dies. This cycle is driven by the concept of "Asabiyyah" (social cohesion or tribal solidarity). He outlined five distinct stages:  
>   
> The Stage of Conquest and Success (The Nomadic Stage): The civilization is born out of strong Asabiyyah, toughness, and a shared struggle. The people are united, and the leader shares power and glory with his tribe to establish the state.  
>   
> The Stage of Autocracy and Consolidation of Power: The ruler begins to monopolize power, distancing himself from the very tribe that helped him rise. He relies on mercenaries and clients to protect his throne, shifting from tribal leadership to absolute monarchy.  
>   
> The Stage of Leisure and Tranquility (Luxury): This is the peak of the civilization. The state is secure, wealth is abundant, and arts, architecture, and the economy flourish. People enjoy the fruits of their ancestors' labor and grow accustomed to luxury.  
>   
> The Stage of Contentment and Peacefulness (Stagnation): The ruler and society become complacent, content with what they have inherited. There is no desire for further expansion or innovation. They adopt a defensive posture, believing their current state will last forever.  
>   
> The Stage of Extravagance and Decline (Senility): The state collapses under the weight of excessive luxury and corruption. Asabiyyah is completely lost, heavy taxes are imposed to fund the extravagant lifestyles of the elite, and the military weakens. The civilization becomes highly vulnerable to being conquered by a new, vigorous group from the periphery with strong Asabiyyah, thus restarting the cycle.

It used a very small number of late manuscripts, some passages were added centuries after Jesus Erasmus (who compiled it) sometimes back-translated from Latin into Greek because he lacked Greek manuscripts.  
>   
> Example: John 5:7 (the “Comma Johanneum”) Explicitly supports the Trinity, not found in the earliest Greek manuscripts, almost certainly a later doctrinal insertion.  Modern Bibles usually remove or footnote this.   
> THIS IS THE BIG ONE: Political influence: King James had an agenda. King James I authorized the translation during intense religious conflict. He wanted to support royal authority, weaken radical Protestant movements, and preserve a hierarchical church structure.  
> Greek words meaning community (ἐκκλησία) :translated as “church” instead of “assembly”  
> Greek episkopos: “bishop” instead of “overseer”

Interesting how Piketty's phases correspond to the phases of enshittification of a startup. First, you build for the consumers. When you have as many users as you can, you use that leverage to get investors. You build for the investors. In the end, when you have investment secured, you build to get acquired. Your focus goes: Consumers -> Investors -> Monopolists. With each step, your product becomes more "enshittified".

> ​@jasmeinaliyeva8992 the offspring of the solitary ones remained social even if the environment was poor and food limited. No cannibalism they genuinely wanted to pass down the gene pool intact. The social ones with multiple environments created to simulate the natural habitat, temperature, day and night cycle multiple feedings daily with multiple setups would establish hierarchs. It was very disappointing because this project was decades of work just to see the friendly social animals continually be the ones to show aggression, kill and cannibalize. I even breed the weaker individuals and shunned individuals for a few generations just to see if they would be less aggressive to each other in what should have been social animals. The weaker and shunned individuals offspring would not be aggressive or cannibalize each other. The solitary predators and social predators would show aggression but no cannibalism and no killings compared to the non predators. Before 2017 I was going to release these experiments but society has all but collapsed. I was heavily involved with society in regards to being paid to do what l do for my personal ambitions for businesses, organizations, private, zoo's etc.  My fears resurfaced around 2017. Something happened with people and it terrified me to the point of dropping clients and family to further my experiments. The conclusion is what we consider a dangerous organism usually has a higher capacity to nurture offspring and respect boundaries of the other dangerous organisms. The more passive or peaceful the organism the more chaos involved with nurturing offspring. The environment could be perfect for them but it didn't matter. In my mice vs rat expirement. Over half of about 2,000 mice killed each other including eating offspring or offspring killing offspring. The rats were angels even the ones purchased from commercial pet stores. Many occasions the rat groups would share food with the weakest individuals or protect sick individuals. They only live a few years at most and whenever they lost one of the members they would grieve and die. The caiman were aggressive as hell towards me the first few years but I managed to breed some of the offspring and they became a bit more tame over time. They were respectful towards each other same with the snakes. I could keep different species of snakes in breeding colonies with no issues with aggression. The piranha's are highly canabalistic when first born regardless of space I fixed that by introducing other predators and the babies packed up in groups in fear of the other predatorial species which typically were turtles 😂. The only thing that would tame humanity at the moment would be an outside threat that everyone could tangibly see or be touched by. > **@GoingUpToTsiyon**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=Ugw6wOmgav_XNX-RxA54AaABAg.AMxwRWJPfEkAQkVJagyyQP)  
>
> I've seen two of the guy's videos. Dozens of comments on each video are cookie-cutter copies of your comment. However, none of these self-styled "critical thinkers" were able to give a single example of what they are referring to without resorting to vaguery.  

> **@arpita1shrivas** | 👍 1 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=Ugw6wOmgav_XNX-RxA54AaABAg.AMxwRWJPfEkAS5smaEcGQT)  
>
> @GoingUpToTsiyon This will be long. It is only for those who are educated, or who consider themselves informed, or who wish to be informed. If you do not want that, skip this. If you are serious about testing ideas, then read carefully.
  
> 
  
> Professor Jiang begins this lecture by pointing to real signs of stress in modern societies. It is true that debt burdens are rising, that birthrates are declining, that inflation eats into wages, that trust is eroding, and that climate stress is visible. None of that is invented. But notice what he does with those truths. He weaves them into a single story of inevitable collapse, strips out complexity, and then positions himself as the only one who can decode the pattern. That is not neutral teaching. That is classic framing for dependence.
  
> 
  
> He says that Thomas Piketty showed capitalism naturally shifts into financialization where speculation overwhelms real wealth creation. It is true that Piketty documents inequality cycles and the growth of capital relative to income. But Piketty does not claim that speculation makes collapse inevitable. He shows that inequality rises unless societies intervene through taxation, regulation, or redistribution. (Piketty, Capital in the Twenty-First Century, Introduction, p. 20). Jiang strips away the agency. Karl Popper warned that this move is historicism, the false belief that history unfolds by scientific law. (Popper, The Open Society and Its Enemies, ch. i, p. 7). Piketty calls for democratic policy choices, not despair. Jiang twists him into a prophet of doom to feed the myth of inevitability.
  
> 
  
> He then moves to Peter Turchin’s idea of elite overproduction. Turchin does argue that when elites multiply faster than positions, instability rises. (Turchin, War and Peace and War, ch. 1, p. 5). But Turchin also stresses the role of asabiya, or collective solidarity, as a counterforce that renews societies. (Turchin, War and Peace and War, ch. 2, p. 44). Jiang ignores solidarity and presents only zero-sum collapse. His “rat utopia” parable based on John Calhoun’s experiments also distorts. Calhoun’s work showed crowding effects in rodents, but anthropologists and sociologists caution against directly mapping this to human societies because humans build institutions, culture, and values that shape behavior beyond raw density. (Douglas, Purity and Danger, ch. 2, p. 41). Jiang uses Calhoun as a scare story to prop up his claim that status fights always end in mutual destruction. That is not what Turchin or serious social science concludes.
  
> 
  
> His third source is Oswald Spengler. Spengler did describe civilizations as organisms that grow, mature, and die. (Spengler, The Decline of the West, Vol. I, p. 21). But even Spengler admitted his morphology was metaphorical, not a law of nature. Historians like Arnold Toynbee critiqued Spengler precisely for this fatalism. Toynbee argued that civilizations decline not from natural death but from a failure of creative minorities to answer challenges. (Toynbee, A Study of History, Vol. I, p. 71). Jiang borrows Spengler’s fatalism but ignores Toynbee’s corrective. By teaching that mega-cities guarantee death, he erases the possibility of renewal and frames collapse as destiny.
  
> 
  
> When challenged about external threats uniting societies, Jiang dismisses the idea. He insists that by the time collapse comes, people are too selfish to respond. That is one reading, but it contradicts history. World War II saw Britain, the Soviet Union, and the United States overcome internal division to fight a common enemy. Hannah Arendt warned that totalitarian systems thrive by atomizing citizens into loneliness. (Arendt, The Origins of Totalitarianism, ch. Three, p. 138). Jiang’s claim that cooperation is impossible is itself propaganda, because it discourages unity and resistance. Jacques Ellul explained that propaganda uses myth and fatalism to condition people into passivity. (Ellul, Propaganda, ch. IV, p. 21). Jiang is not simply analyzing, he is integrating his audience into a worldview where resignation looks like wisdom.
  
> 
  
> His model of elites, managers, and workers as a corporation is also revealing. He says the owners are the families, the managers are the professional class, and the people are the workers. In his story, managers always betray the workers, and collapse is inevitable once elites produce too many children. But this ignores long historical evidence of reform. Popper stressed piecemeal social engineering as the alternative to utopian despair. (Popper, The Open Society and Its Enemies, ch. 3, p. 23). Democracies have reformed banking systems, limited monopolies, and expanded rights. None of that fits Jiang’s model, so he discards it. He prefers a parable of betrayal because it suits his theme.
  
> 
  
> When he concludes by predicting that America and Europe will lose freedom, collapse economically, descend into civil conflict, and fight “stupid foreign wars,” the pattern is clear. He began with true stresses, then imported distorted readings of Piketty, Turchin, and Spengler, stripped away agency, and ended in despair. That structure is not education. It is propaganda. Lifton described how totalist environments end with “dispensing of existence,” where those outside the doctrine are treated as blind or corrupt. (Lifton, Thought Reform and the Psychology of Totalism, ch. 9, p. 179). By casting Western societies as doomed, Jiang primes his students to see themselves as the elect few who see clearly, while everyone else is blind.
  
> 
  
> To be fair, it is true that inequality, debt, and demographic decline are real problems. It is true that elites can crowd out opportunity, and that societies do face cycles of expansion and crisis. But the distortion lies in how Jiang presents these as unbreakable laws. He does not invite reform, he does not credit resilience, he does not admit creative minorities. He gives a myth that collapses agency and flatters only his audience.
  
> 
  
> You can decide whether Jiang is a CCP agitator, a wannabe cult leader, delusional, or simply narcissistic. What is clear is that his calm professor mask does not change the structure. He begins with real problems, builds a myth of inevitability, and ends by binding students to him as the decoder. He is not simply lecturing on collapse. In his own words he wants to build a new intellectual movement, to practice “predictive history,” to become a kind of Hari Seldon who alone can foresee the future and train an order of intellectual “Jedis” (Meet Professor Jiang video). 
  
> This is not history, it is self-anointment. YouTube is his true classroom, and the audience is his true target. The method is not education but thought reform. The danger is not only that his model of collapse is wrong, but that he uses it to collapse independent thinking and recruit followers into his fantasy of being a messianic prophet of history.  
>

We are not confined to a single location-hence the migration phenomenon-which is very old indeed, having started out of Africa some multiple millions of years ago.  Human movement is also related much more to access to resources like food, shelter and opportunity than it is to finding mates.  (Mexicans do not come to the US to find mates.)  4. The whole "alpha male model" has been overturned and is largely men projecting the psyche of SOME men  onto the natural world.  The "hierarchies" of nature, where they do exist, are much more subtle and complex.

### **@Dugout97** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=Ugx8mMFxXJ4UJPv_47N4AaABAg)

This will be long. It is only for those who are educated, or who consider themselves informed, or who wish to be informed. If you do not want that, skip this. If you are serious about testing ideas, then read carefully.

Professor Jiang begins this lecture by pointing to real signs of stress in modern societies. It is true that debt burdens are rising, that birthrates are declining, that inflation eats into wages, that trust is eroding, and that climate stress is visible. None of that is invented. But notice what he does with those truths. He weaves them into a single story of inevitable collapse, strips out complexity, and then positions himself as the only one who can decode the pattern. That is not neutral teaching. That is classic framing for dependence.

He says that Thomas Piketty showed capitalism naturally shifts into financialization where speculation overwhelms real wealth creation. It is true that Piketty documents inequality cycles and the growth of capital relative to income. But Piketty does not claim that speculation makes collapse inevitable. He shows that inequality rises unless societies intervene through taxation, regulation, or redistribution. (Piketty, Capital in the Twenty-First Century, Introduction, p. 20). Jiang strips away the agency. Karl Popper warned that this move is historicism, the false belief that history unfolds by scientific law. (Popper, The Open Society and Its Enemies, ch. i, p. 7). Piketty calls for democratic policy choices, not despair. Jiang twists him into a prophet of doom to feed the myth of inevitability.

He then moves to Peter Turchin’s idea of elite overproduction. Turchin does argue that when elites multiply faster than positions, instability rises. (Turchin, War and Peace and War, ch. 1, p. 5). But Turchin also stresses the role of asabiya, or collective solidarity, as a counterforce that renews societies. (Turchin, War and Peace and War, ch. 2, p. 44). Jiang ignores solidarity and presents only zero-sum collapse. His “rat utopia” parable based on John Calhoun’s experiments also distorts. Calhoun’s work showed crowding effects in rodents, but anthropologists and sociologists caution against directly mapping this to human societies because humans build institutions, culture, and values that shape behavior beyond raw density. (Douglas, Purity and Danger, ch. 2, p. 41). Jiang uses Calhoun as a scare story to prop up his claim that status fights always end in mutual destruction. That is not what Turchin or serious social science concludes.

His third source is Oswald Spengler. Spengler did describe civilizations as organisms that grow, mature, and die. (Spengler, The Decline of the West, Vol. I, p. 21). But even Spengler admitted his morphology was metaphorical, not a law of nature. Historians like Arnold Toynbee critiqued Spengler precisely for this fatalism. Toynbee argued that civilizations decline not from natural death but from a failure of creative minorities to answer challenges. (Toynbee, A Study of History, Vol. I, p. 71). Jiang borrows Spengler’s fatalism but ignores Toynbee’s corrective. By teaching that mega-cities guarantee death, he erases the possibility of renewal and frames collapse as destiny.

When challenged about external threats uniting societies, Jiang dismisses the idea. He insists that by the time collapse comes, people are too selfish to respond. That is one reading, but it contradicts history. World War II saw Britain, the Soviet Union, and the United States overcome internal division to fight a common enemy. Hannah Arendt warned that totalitarian systems thrive by atomizing citizens into loneliness. (Arendt, The Origins of Totalitarianism, ch. Three, p. 138). Jiang’s claim that cooperation is impossible is itself propaganda, because it discourages unity and resistance. Jacques Ellul explained that propaganda uses myth and fatalism to condition people into passivity. (Ellul, Propaganda, ch. IV, p. 21). Jiang is not simply analyzing, he is integrating his audience into a worldview where resignation looks like wisdom.

His model of elites, managers, and workers as a corporation is also revealing. He says the owners are the families, the managers are the professional class, and the people are the workers. In his story, managers always betray the workers, and collapse is inevitable once elites produce too many children. But this ignores long historical evidence of reform. Popper stressed piecemeal social engineering as the alternative to utopian despair. (Popper, The Open Society and Its Enemies, ch. 3, p. 23). Democracies have reformed banking systems, limited monopolies, and expanded rights. None of that fits Jiang’s model, so he discards it. He prefers a parable of betrayal because it suits his theme.

When he concludes by predicting that America and Europe will lose freedom, collapse economically, descend into civil conflict, and fight “stupid foreign wars,” the pattern is clear. He began with true stresses, then imported distorted readings of Piketty, Turchin, and Spengler, stripped away agency, and ended in despair. That structure is not education. It is propaganda. Lifton described how totalist environments end with “dispensing of existence,” where those outside the doctrine are treated as blind or corrupt. (Lifton, Thought Reform and the Psychology of Totalism, ch. 9, p. 179). By casting Western societies as doomed, Jiang primes his students to see themselves as the elect few who see clearly, while everyone else is blind.

To be fair, it is true that inequality, debt, and demographic decline are real problems. It is true that elites can crowd out opportunity, and that societies do face cycles of expansion and crisis. But the distortion lies in how Jiang presents these as unbreakable laws. He does not invite reform, he does not credit resilience, he does not admit creative minorities. He gives a myth that collapses agency and flatters only his audience.

You can decide whether Jiang is a CCP agitator, a wannabe cult leader, delusional, or simply narcissistic. What is clear is that his calm professor mask does not change the structure. He begins with real problems, builds a myth of inevitability, and ends by binding students to him as the decoder. He is not simply lecturing on collapse. In his own words he wants to build a new intellectual movement, to practice “predictive history,” to become a kind of Hari Seldon who alone can foresee the future and train an order of intellectual “Jedis” (Meet Professor Jiang video). 
This is not history, it is self-anointment. YouTube is his true classroom, and the audience is his true target. The method is not education but thought reform. The danger is not only that his model of collapse is wrong, but that he uses it to collapse independent thinking and recruit followers into his fantasy of being a messianic prophet of history.

This was a fascinating lecture. I appreciated how Professor Jiang tied together financialization, elite overproduction, and civilizational life cycles into a single framework. The signs of decline he listed feel very visible today.

That said, I think the prediction of a sweeping global collapse may be too deterministic. History shows that empires and systems often unravel unevenly, with localized collapses and power shifts rather than one sudden, universal breakdown. The U.S. in particular shows signs of overreach and imperial hubris, but moving forward its decline may take the form of a hollowing out and fragmentation rather than a total collapse all at once.

The framework is still valuable though, it helps us see the patterns, even if the outcomes differ across regions.

### **@hivemind5132** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=UgxVagQNCJLcGwqQy894AaABAg)

It's built on the theories of Ibn Khaldun and Ibn Rushd, such as the state being a living and dying organism or the addition of personality. Their names were never mentioned in class, which is a bit odd. The model you're describing resembles an artistocracy, but there's a problem: the Ottomans had a monarchy, but to prevent the emergence of an aristocracy, they killed their brothers. They lived for 600 years, but that didn't exactly prevent their downfall. After they entered decline, they stopped killing their brothers. The Abbasids survived for over 500 years without using this method. This can be interpreted as the state as a whole reaching its limits, rather than the founders reaching their limits, as was the interpretation of the Muslim scholars who proposed the theory. What limited these limits regarding the collapse of the Ottoman Empire was the fact that Europeans were able to expand their global fraud and exploitation network with the financing they obtained from different continents. In the 18th and 19th centuries, Ottoman society began to fall behind Europe in terms of population size, and people in Europe began to produce many more children.

### **@ameeralward6769**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=UgzkZKmRBBW1KNOzocl4AaABAg)

22:02 
For historical context, the most brilliant and systematic theory of the civilizational life cycle was articulated by the Arab polymath Ibn Khaldun (1332–1406) in his masterpiece, the Muqaddimah.

Ibn Khaldun's theory is striking for its depth:

The Driving Force: He argued that the cycle is driven by 'Asabiyyah (Group Cohesion/Social Solidarity). He observed that states are founded by hardy, nomadic groups with strong 'Asabiyyah.

The Inevitable Decay: Once established in the urban centers, this cohesion inevitably weakens over five predictable stages as the population grows accustomed to luxury and decadence.

The Outcome: The dynasty eventually loses the essential 'Asabiyyah needed to defend itself, making its collapse and replacement by a new, cohesive group not just a failure, but a sociological inevitability.

Ibn Khaldun effectively created a universal, law-governed framework for the rise and fall of states centuries before Spengler, cementing his place as a foundational figure in sociology and the true originator of this theory.

I have great respect for the professor and I really appreciate his work. I just wanted to share a small concern as someone who is trying to learn. Around 29:00, I felt that the student’s question was slightly misunderstood – as far as I could tell, the student was referring to the Venus Project.

Of course, the professor’s ideas are valuable, but the concepts are quite advanced, and I worry that many students and viewers (myself included) may struggle to fully follow them. From the outside, this can sometimes make the lecture feel more like a one-way persuasion than a mutual discussion.

Similarly, around 42:00, it seemed to me that the professor did not fully engage with the question from the student’s own perspective and instead remained within his own framework. This is completely understandable given the limits of time and format, but I still think it is worth mentioning.

I’m not saying this as a hostile criticism, but as a sincere observation from someone who is also learning a lot from these lectures. When such strong and complex ideas are made fully open and accessible to everyone, there is also a risk that they may be misunderstood or taken in a very simplified way. I just want us, as listeners, to stay conscious and critical so that we can benefit from the lectures as much as possible. 0:03

Dear Professor Jiang.
If people don't trust each other, it isn't that they can't, but rather that there are artificial limitations preventing people from proving their trust, worth, or power. 
An external threat will not solve the problem, but a common goal, such as an external threat, as long as the civilisation's abstractions are 'valid', will.
Hipocricy: For example, if people understand the basics, but cannot improve it, they lack 'courage' (of the menial kind), and then, they cannot practice what they preach.
Delegation: If people cannot raise their past selves, they are unable to reach their future potential. And as such, cannot repeat what they practice.
Etymology: Things always happen for a reason, and civilisations always fail for a reason. With a question is an answer, an a problem is also a nascent solution - the origin of all things is Named, and the Name of All Things is Original. By clarifying the existence of things, we can isolate them from other things, bringing forth innovation. This innovation is not 'bound' by other labels, and vices or false incentives cannot prevent this Language of Infinite Progress - that spans every civilisation, and makes civilisations stand out from other processees in the same infinitely nested epicycle, such as evolution or entropy.
Epilogue: People cannot 'repeat' a cycle individually, but overall, civilisations (and everything, all at once) have infinite meta-cycles, that eventaully reach a higher abstraction than the last. This completely changes (and reframes) your 'zero sum' world into a positive one. Additiaonlly, a smaller epicycle will feed-back into higher cycles, inifinitely reverberating until a satisfying conclusion (of the correct harmonic or otherwise chordant reverberant-echoing 'note'), is reached. This fundamentally rewrites your negative absolute into a positive absolute - nothing can go wrong, everything has a reason, and success is just a different type of failure!a

If history really follows these cyclical patterns, does this push us toward a kind of “historical Stoicism,” where the task is to understand and accept the cycles rather than imagine we can fully escape them?

Your model also raises more metaphysical questions: if the same structural mistakes recur with different actors, does this suggest that “time” is less linear than we think, or even that we are in a kind of simulation where the same dualities and conflicts replay under new guises?

And finally, if these patterns are so persistent at the level of civilizations, do you see any meaningful analogy to ideas of reincarnation at the level of societies—civilizations “coming back” in new forms—even if individuals do not?

Best,
Antonio

### **@fearful-of-missing-out**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=UgzG-XSOFF0Vpf_Iirh4AaABAg)

"Three types of social formulae seem plausible in the light of the history of the world-system. One is a sort of neo-feudalism that would reproduce in a far more equilibrated form the developments of the time of troubles – a world of parcellized sovereignties, or considerably more autarkic regions, of local hierarchies. This might be made compatible with maintaining (but probably not furthering) the current relatively high level of technology. Endless accumulation of capital could no longer function as the mainspring of such a system, but it would certainly be an inegalitarian system. What would legitimate it? Perhaps a return to a belief in natural hierarchies. A second formula might be a sort of democratic fascism. Such a formula would involve a caste-like division of the world into two strata, the top one incorporating perhaps a fifth of the world’s population. Within this stratum, there could be a high degree of egalitarian distribution. On the basis of such a community of interests within such a large group, they might have the strength to keep the other 80 percent in the position of a totally disarmed working proletariat. Hitler’s new world order had such a vision in mind, but then it defined itself in terms of too narrow a top stratum. A third formula might be a still more radical worldwide highly decentralized, highly egalitarian world order. This seems the most utopian of the three but it is scarcely to be ruled out. Are there still other possibilities? Of course there are. What is important to recognize is that all three historical options are really there, and the choice will depend on our collective world behavior over the next fifty years. Whichever option is chosen, it will not be the end of history, but in a real sense its beginning. The human social world is still very young in cosmological time. In 2050 or 2100, when we look back at capitalist civilization, what will we think?" — Immanuel Wallerstein, Historical Capitalism with Capitalist Civilization (1981)

Also, regions such as the Middle East and North Africa, sub-Saharan Africa, and South Asia tend to have the lowest levels of freedom. On the other side, the most open countries in terms of freedom, according to recent 2024/2025 reports, are largely clustered in Europe and Oceania, with Switzerland, New Zealand, and Denmark. But I still agree that the US is in decline, as Ray Dalio explained at length. So is the west, but also the rest of the world. No matter what, humans have reached a stage where technological development and urbanization is threatening not only human civilization but life on earth. This is well explained in the book Sapiens: A Brief History of Humankind from historian Yuval Noah Harari. New challenges are emerging with AI, affecting the middle class of blue collar workers. Indeed, we are at the intersection of many looming crises, economic, financial, environmental, technological, social and demographic. What would really help is to focus on possible solutions in order to have some hope in the future and how to deal with the next 5 to 20 years.

### **@PaulCharles-k3rsz** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=K-_l9jBGo74&lc=UgyQ4pZeomcJA7H3pGl4AaABAg)

The final stages of capitalism , Karl Marx wrote, will be marked by developments  that are intimately familiar.
Unable to expand and generate profits at past levels, the capitalist system will begin to consume the structures that sustain it.
In the name of austerity and government efficiency it will prey upon the working class and the poor driving them ever deeper into debt and poverty and diminishing the capacity of the state to serve the needs of ordinary citizens.
It will, as it has, increasingly relocate jobs including both manufacturing and professional positions to countries with cheap pools of labour.
Industries will mechanise their workplaces.
This will trigger an economic assault on not only the working class but the middle class, the  bullwark of a capitalist system. 
This will at first be disguised by the imposition of massive personal debt as incomes decline or remain stagnant.
Politics in the late stage of capitalism will become subordinate to economics, leading to political parties hollowed out of any real political content and objectly subservient to the dictates of corporations and oligarchs.
There is a limit to an economy built upon the scaffolding of debt expansion.
There comes a moment when there will be no new markets available and no new pools of people who can take on more debt. 
Capitalism will then turn on the so-called free market itself along with the values and traditions it claims to defend. 
It will in its final stages it will pillage the very systems and structures that make capitalism possible. 
Causing widespread suffering it will resort to harsher forms of oppression.
In a frantic last stand it will attempt to maintain its profits by looting and pillaging state institutions in contradiction of its stated nature.
The final stages of captitalism are not capitalism at all, but merely hubris, desperation, obscene selfishness  and insatiable greed.
Corporations gobble down government expenditures (taxpayer money) like pigs at a trough.
Then the system crashes.

I disagree with 44:31. China and the United were very different in terms of freedom of expression around this era. 

In the US Freedom of expression was viewed as an inalienable right protected by the First Amendment. Even during the "Red Scare," the Supreme Court eventually stepped in to limit the government’s ability to punish speech.

Meanwhile in China Freedom of expression was viewed as a tool for the state. The 1954 Constitution technically "guaranteed" free speech, but in practice, it was a privilege reserved only for "the people" (those who supported the Revolution). Anyone defined by the Party as an "enemy" or "counter-revolutionary" was stripped of all rights.

For example look into the Anti-Rightist Campaign
